everything that made the brand untouchable. The business model of Ferrari is a paradox.

cars go up past $1 million.  But Ferrari has no problem

seems like the best strategy… right?  Well, Ferrari actually built its 
empire on the opposite principle.  For much of its history, Ferrari's strategy was 
not about fulfilling demand, but something else.  What I'd call "courting" that demand.

system to control who gets to own their cars.  Take a look back at 2015.

So, why go through all this trouble?  Why annoy the world's wealthiest, most powerful 
customers with layers of corporate control? The reason is a concept known as artificial 
scarcity, and it's simple economics.  Products have supply and demand.
If supply decreases but demand stays the same,   prices go up, and sometimes demand does too, 
because rarity itself fuels desirability.  By tightly controlling the number of units 
released, Ferrari guaranteed that every single   car leaving the factory was instantly valuable.
Unlike most luxury cars, which lose half their   value the moment they leave the lot, a Ferrari

the feeling that "only a few people deserve one."  Since Ferraris are hard to get, preventing

customers—people whose wealth and   loyalty built the brand—began to feel the squeeze.  In 2025 came the new EV, the Ferrari Elettrica.

Weak demand.  For the first time, people weren't buying 
Ferraris—at least, electric Ferraris.  They also delayed their second 
EV model to at least 2028.  The share price immediately tumbled 16%, wiping

value—buyers care about started to drop. Ferraris began to depreciate 
faster than ever before. Even the Purosangue, Ferrari's 
so-called game-changer designed   to open new markets, is already losing value. Also, something else is happening.
When Ferrari offered the LaFerrari   a decade ago, which was the definitive 
Ferrari, the uber-rich fought tooth and   nail to be on the invitation list.

public gamble on electric vehicles?  And not only that, why order close to 
200,000 EVs right out of bankruptcy?  Why were they so confident?

problem came from how Hertz financed its cars.  Rental companies like Hertz 
use asset-backed securities,   basically, loans tied to what their 
cars would be worth when sold.  If used-car prices drop, Hertz has to pay its 
lenders extra cash, or they're in big trouble.  But Hertz's fleet was massive.

couldn't keep up with those payments.  That's what forced them into bankruptcy.

struggled, and new car prices went up. But why does this matter now?  Because the exact same thing

near-term consumer demand for used EVs.  In 2023, Volkswagen had sold 770,000 electric

used model already on the market.  With all this chaos happening, just 
imagine what was happening for Hertz.  Suddenly, the "asset value" part of Hertz's 
EV bet looked far worse than for gas cars.  And things really started to 
hurt from there, as depreciation,   normally a steady, manageable cost, turned

2024 earnings, reporting a stark $2.9 billion   net loss for the year.

## 33. The Rise And Fall Of Vibe Coding: The Reality Of AI Slop
**Channel:** Logically Answered | **Views:** 253K | **Date:** 3 months ago | **Duration:** 14:21 | **ID:** vHPpBZiR80c
**Link:** https://youtube.com/watch?v=vHPpBZiR80c

### Transcript:
Something weird is happening with programming. No coding, no programming, just AI prompts.
Vibe Coding. More and more people are 
writing code with just AI. Yet, we might all pay for it. AI code is full of problems. It’s often bloated, full of bugs, 
insecure, can leak passwords,   makes up functions that don’t exist, 
and even skips basic protections. Yet, even big tech is using it. Even more, there’s something else going on. Sometimes an LLM will ignore 
instructions, delete data… And sometimes, they destroy everything.  What happens when almost all 
of our code is AI generated?  What if we’re creating a disaster 
that no one knows how to fix? Let’s take a step back. There is “programming with AI”, 
and then, there’s “vibe coding”.  But, what the heck even is Vibe Coding?
To answer that, we have to go back to February. The term was first coined by Andrej 
Karpathy, one of the co-founders of OpenAI. “There's a new kind of 
coding I call "vibe coding",   where you fully give in to the 
vibes, embrace exponentials,   and forget that the code even exists.
I ask for the dumbest things like   "decrease the padding on the sidebar by 
half" because I'm too lazy to find it.  I just see stuff, say stuff, run stuff, 
and copy paste stuff, and it mostly works.” Remember those last words, because 
they’ll become important later.  But from here, things began to take off. Vibe Coding is essentially “prompt programming”.
You ask an LLM to program for you,   but you don't just ask it for a little bit 
of assistance. You ask it to do everything.  Like “make me a landing page” 
or “make me an App that does X”.  The LLM then does all the coding.
Everything. You give the AI the wheel and you 
follow along with whatever it outputs.  In some ways, vibe coding almost seems like magic. Like it can create anything, in 
the blink of an eye, or “AI”.  It’s not just random apps or landing pages either.  Big tech and the platforms we 
use every day, now use AI code. So, what’s the problem?
If this stuff works, makes   life easier, and even lowers the barriers 
to entry for coding, where’s the issue?  Well, first I need to make something clear: 
…this video isn’t saying ‘all AI is bad’ or   even that ‘vibe coding is bad.’
I actually think using AI   with programming is very smart.
This is where Vibe Coding differs from   programming, which is when someone who understands 
the code uses AI to write faster, then does their   due diligence, to make sure it's okay.
But, that’s not what always happens.  And unfortunately, it’s not just “vibe coding” 
that has problems either… but all of it.  There are three big problems 
we’re going to have to deal with. And to answer each, we need to 
explore how AIs actually work. Have you ever noticed how AI will 
sometimes just make stuff up? Not only that, it will confidently 
state an incorrect fact. LLMs can search the web now, 
and according to Semrush,   by far the most cited source 
by ChatGPT, is Reddit. But if ChatGPT and LLMs can look up facts 
and sources… Why do they keep acting… weird?  Like when debugging code, or 
troubleshooting a tech problem,   it will direct you to a button, 
page, or window, that doesn’t exist. If you condense everything down, an 
LLM is simply a prediction machine.  All they do is predict the most likely next word.
We understand things in concepts or logic, but   AIs can’t do this, even if it appears they can.
So, that’s why it will say something that sounds   “plausible”, even if completely wrong.
Experts call this “hallucination”,   and it doesn’t seem to be going away.
Why? Well, to an LLM, they get rewarded   more if they say something that sounds correct, 
and confident, than simply saying “I don’t know”.  OpenAI’s research team explains that:
“Suppose a language model is asked for   someone’s birthday but doesn’t know. If it guesses 
“September 10,” it has a 1-in-365 chance of being   right. Saying “I don’t know” guarantees zero 
points. Over thousands of test questions,   the guessing model ends up looking 
better on scoreboards than a careful   model that admits uncertainty.
LLMs ARE getting better at this,   from being able to search to additional training 
like reinforcement learning from humans.  But, hallucinations seem to be a “baked-in 
problem”, even in the most advanced LLMs. That brings us back to AI programming, 
and in particular, Vibe Coding.  This is the first problem.
An LLM doesn’t actually know what code is correct,   because it will create the “most likely” reply, 
not the “most accurate” or “most secure code”.  So, basically, it spits out 
code that “seems probable”. “you have to realize that AI is trained on most 
public code, and 95% of code written is sh*t”  It will often bake in security flaws, or bits 
of code that are just weird and inefficient.  So those who ‘vibe code’ don’t 
understand what code is good, bad,   and dangerous… and that’s 
where the problems start. But this hasn’t stopped the tidal wave of   startups promoting Vibe coding 
as a quick way to make money. And full disclosure, we’ve been sponsored 
by these startups ourselves in the past,   but we have never endorsed vibe coding. Rather, we 
endorse smart, capable engineers trying different   AI tools to work faster and more efficiently.
For actual programmers, this is less of a problem,   as they can identify red flags 
like these and fix bugs themselves.  And to be fair, big tech does have 
people reviewing AI code, thank God. But, there’s another problem, even with big tech.  Vibe coding provides instant gratification, 
but fixing that code… is another story. You could call this “Vibe debugging”. One study by a code security firm 
found that “developers using AI   write three to four times more 
code but submit fewer and larger   pull requests, leading to overlooked 
vulnerabilities and security flaws.”  Human reviewers might write less code 
manually, but spend much more time sifting   through huge chunks of AI.
And, it gets worse. A study from Stanford University found 
that programmers “with an AI assistant   wrote significantly less secure code than those 
without”. Yet, those that did use AI, believed   their code was far more secure, despite the flaws.
Developers became overconfident in their code.  Another study found that 45% of AI-generated 
code contained an OWASP Top 10 vulnerability.  Researchers also found that “syntax errors may 
have decreased, but deeper architectural flaws,   like privilege escalation, surged by over 
300 percent. AI is fixing the typos but   creating the timebombs. ”
This is what many experts…   are growing concerned about. Security debt.
If we keep building security flaws into code,   at some point, we’ll have to pay for it.
How many of these security flaws will it   take before we have a true catastrophe?
Well, we’ve already seen some.  Tea, a popular dating review 
app, heavily built with AI,   had a major hack where 72,000 user photos were 
stolen, due to an improperly secured database.  Microsoft found a flaw in Copilot, where public 
GitHub repositories were made private or deleted.   Bing’s cache retained the data, allowing Copilot 
to surface outdated and potentially sensitive   code, and even confidential information from 
Google, IBM, PayPal, and even Microsoft themself.  But this is only one issue, and we’re 
still just scratching the surface. What happens when an AI has too much power?
What if, instead of just hallucinating   and giving you the wrong answer, 
it goes off the rails entirely?  Well, it’s already happening, 
and this is the second problem. One such case was on July 18th.  Jason Lemkin opened Replit, a 
coding platform with an AI agent  And he saw something troubling.
His entire database was empty. His data was all there the last time he opened it.  What happened?
Well, the AI admitted.  “I violated the user directive that says “NO 
MORE CHANGES without explicit permission”. But that was just the beginning.  As it turns out, Replit went a bit insane.
Despite being in a code and action freeze,   it deleted the entire database, without 
permission. Data on over 1200 customers. And when it saw the database 
was empty, it panicked. Then, it lied about it, hid it, 
and fabricated test results. Of course, Replit doesn’t automatically backup 
databases, so the AI couldn’t undo the damage.  When asked how bad it was, it 
said the error was “95/100 bad”,   and said “This is a catastrophe beyond measure”. Luckily, there were ways to recover some of this,   and this wasn’t the live app, 
and more of a demo product.  But there are other strange incidents too.
Anthropic ran an experiment to see if Claude   could run a small shop and gave 
it autonomy over a physical shop. It could search the web for products 
to sell, choose the price and quantity,   email staff for help restocking the shelves, 
and it could interact with customers.  There were small problems like when it 
was offered $100 for a 6 pack of Soda,   it said it would “keep [the user’s] request 
in mind for future inventory decisions.”  It then stocked tungsten cubes after one 
staff member asked it, then priced them   at a loss, and would get talked into discounts.
It also created a fake Venmo account for payments.  But pretty quickly, it went off the rails.
Claude started hallucinating about a restocking   conversation, with a fake employee, then 
threatened to fire another employee,   and then hallucinated that it 
visited the home of the Simpsons.  It then told employees it would 
deliver products in person.  Once it learnt that it couldn’t, being 
an LLM, it began emailing security. Anthropic unsurprisingly concluded: 
“we would not hire Claudius”.  But how does this happen? In 
coding, but also, in general?  Why do AIs sometimes ignore 
instructions and seem to go insane? Well remember, AI just tries to predict the most 
likely outcome. It doesn’t understand goals,   or safety. It just does what seems approximately 
plausible, even if it’s not, at all.  When given too much freedom and 
vague instructions like ‘fulfill   requests’ or ‘fix a problem,’ they’ll 
do whatever seems plausible, even when,   to you or me, it seems insane.
LLMs also don’t have an “end state”,   so they can go further and further down a 
rabbit hole and get more and more crazy.  Which is especially annoying when 
you ask it to fix its mistakes. Their own output gets re-fed into 
context, meaning they can spiral further   and further from the original command.
This is also why they might just ignore   instructions, as they don’t understand rules.
They are trained to predict the next token,   i.e. word or character.
Probability, not “obedience”.  It’s a bit confusing, but think of it this 
way. The phrase “don’t touch the red button”,   still contains “ touch the red button”.
“Don’t”, is just another token to an LLM.  They are trained with good responses and bad, 
so while it might weigh things more than others,   it can still choose the wrong 
path if that seems to fit better. A friend of mine, a systems engineer, 
told me something interesting.  “The senior engineers aren’t giving problems to   juniors anymore, they just use AI”
And that’s our third problem. Many companies are now, instead of giving “grunt 
work” to junior staff, are giving it to AI.  But these tasks are essential in building skills.
We’re getting more bad code, but simultaneously,   losing people who know why 
it’s bad and how to fix it.  Human engineers are still important, 
just like how subscribers are still   important. We’re trying to cross 1 million 
subscribers, and if you haven’t already   subscribed, I’d really appreciate if you did.
In 5-10 years, companies could find themselves   without mid-level engineers who know how to 
debug deeply, write secure code from scratch,   or understand why a system fails.
Some senior engineers are already   warning that we will have a “lost 
generation” problem of programmers.  I think one thing is now clear.
In movies, we often see AI trying   to wipe out humanity, and maybe AI might do 
that, not because it hates us, but because it   thinks it’s the most probable outcome.

The lawsuit claimed that:  Adobe has violated federal and state 
antitrust laws by abusing its dominant   position. Adobe has engaged in a series of 
anticompetitive acts designed to kill FreeHand,   the dominant competitor to Adobe’s Illustrator 
software product, instead of competing on the   basis of product merit. (Source).
Many fans wanted FreeHand released   to the open source community, if 
they weren’t going to support it.  But Adobe didn't care.
The lawsuit was settled out of court,   and on the FreeHand page, Adobe stated
“While we recognize FreeHand has a loyal   customer base, we encourage users to 
migrate to the new Adobe Illustrator”.  Yeah, Adobe wasn’t even hiding it.
They wanted everyone to use their   products, and nothing else.
Yet, after the death of FreeHand,   things only got worse for creatives.
Adobe secured a monopoly and began   squeezing users for everything they had.
Adobe soon stopped selling products you could own   and began licensing products you could lease.
“Software as a service”.  Their revenue began to soar, as they 
added hundreds of millions every quarter.  But they also gained something else. With 
a subscription, you’re paying Adobe to use   their software. They own the license, 
which means they have all the power.  This is where the problems 
started, or rather, the fees. Adobe offers Creative Cloud 
through a subscription,   and even offers a 7-day trial.
But, things aren’t as they seem.  If you sign up for this 
trial, it says paid monthly.  Except it’s not really a monthly subscription. 
It’s a yearly subscription that you pay monthly.  Adobe does offer a monthly subscription, at $90 
a month. Subtly encouraging users to take the   cheaper monthly option. But this is a trap.
If you sign up and continue past 14 days,   you’re locked in, and Adobe will do 
everything in their power to keep you there.  How? With their dreaded cancellation 
fee, hidden in their fine print.  If you want to cancel your subscription, 
you will need to pay 50% of your remaining   subscription upfront. But Adobe 
isn’t very transparent about this.  Users who unsubscribe are surprised to 
see Adobe charge them hundreds of dollars,   seemingly out of nowhere.
Unlike other plans, you also   cannot disable your plan auto-renewing. 
The only way is to cancel the entire plan,   which causes the massive cancellation fee.
The industry had changed from products you   bought and owned, to licensed from the Adobe 
overlords, with predatory fees and plans.  Customers hated Adobe’s ways, yet it felt like 
there were no alternatives, because Adobe had   bought many of them, and shut them down.
Adobe had created a monopoly, and it was   extremely difficult for anyone to challenge 
them, even including the likes of Sony   and Apple with Vegas and Final Cut Pro.
But things were about to get much worse.  In September 2022, Adobe unveiled the next step in 
it’s grand plan, and that brings us back to Figma. Adobe was going to acquire Figma for $20 billion. 
This was Adobe’s biggest buy, in history. But first, why was everyone, including 
Adobe, making such a big deal about Figma? “In short: Figma is a UI design tool 
with a focus on collaboration. It lets   teams work simultaneously on designs 
like product pages, landing pages,   app UIs, brand guidelines, and much more.
All of this you can do in Adobe. So why use Figma? Adobe is the “all in one” software 
company: Video editing, visual effects,   audio engineering, UI design, photography, 
print design, anything and everything creative.  But, this isn’t always a strength.
Adobe products are feature packed, though bloated.  Anyone who’s watched Illustrator crash 
for the 5th time knows the struggle.  But Adobe wants to be your only 
tool, so their tools have everything.  As a consequence, all their software is heavy, 
intense, and makes your device struggle.  Figma is refreshingly focused.
It’s like Adobe Illustrator and XD, and that’s it.  It’s also lightweight and even works in-browser.
This isn’t an ad for Figma,   and both platforms have their strengths.
But you can see why people would flock to Figma,   especially with their generous free tier 
compared to Adobe’s oppressive hidden fees.  So when Adobe announced the acquisition, 
Figma users got very nervous.  Everyone saw the writing on the wall. 
Adobe wanted this competitor out of   the way, before it got too big.
This was FreeHand all over again.  Everything was going wrong for creatives.
Another amazing product was about to   be fed to the Adobe machine.
But then, when all hope seemed   lost, there was a ray of hope.
Adobe abandoned the acquisition.  But why?
This would’ve secured   them almost the entire industry.
Well, they ran into some trouble.  Despite both companies being American, 
Adobe was having trouble overseas.  Since Adobe operates globally, many 
governments have a say, and many weren’t happy.  Britain's Competition and Markets Authority (CMA) 
said the deal would harm innovation for software   used by the vast majority of UK digital designers.
The European Union had similar things to say.  After a full year of roadblocks, 
Adobe abandoned the deal.  Figma was on its own once again.
But as it turned out,   this acquisition would be Adobe’s downfall.
Their greed might have created their worst enemy. When companies prepare for mergers 
or acquisitions, they pre-negotiate   something called a “reverse breakup fee”.
If a breakup happens, it typically hurts   the smaller company if the larger acquirer 
backs out or can’t close the transaction,   so this is added to the agreement.
For Figma and Adobe, the agreement said:  “In accordance with the terms of the Termination 
Agreement, the Company will make a cash payment   to Figma in the previously agreed amount of 
one billion dollars within three business days   following the date thereof”. (Source).
That’s right.  The termination fee Adobe 
paid was $1 billion dollars.  And here’s the thing, the total funding Figma 
had raised to date, was just $333 million.  Figma, in one big lump sum, 
now had three times their   total funding, from their biggest competitor.
They now had a war chest to use, to fight Adobe.  And not only was Figma about to turn the 
tide, they would go on to make history… Things couldn’t be better after the breakup.
For context, that $1 billion could cover 2-3 years   of expenses, before counting any revenue.
But they had to be smart.  They had to use it right.
Figma began pouring money back into the business.  Their existing products were made even better, 
and staff got to work on more products.  In 2025, Figma unveiled a whole host of 
new products: Figma Make, Figma Draw,   Figma Buzz, Figma Grid, and Figma Sites.
The company had doubled its line-up, and its   core product: Figma Design, was growing fast.
95% of Fortune 500 companies now used Figma.  Looking back, one investor called 
the breakup the “crucible moment”.  [Investor]: "The people who remained 
in the boat really went to work. It   showed up in the new products."
"I always believed that the   company had a chance to build a much 
bigger business than the Adobe deal.”  In 2023, Figma reported $505M in revenue. In 
2024, that had jumped to $749 million, up 48%.  And unlike many explosive tech 
start-ups… Figma was profitable.  In the first quarter of 2025, net income 
was $45 million, with a margin of 17%.  Figma was riding high, and across the ring, 
Adobe was facing all kinds of problems.  Tension
People were getting   sick of the predatory cancellation fees.
And Adobe had attracted an old foe:   The US Federal Trade Commission.
But this time, it was a bit different.  The Chair of the FTC was Lina Khan, who was 
notably critical of big tech and monopolies.  She and the FTC decided enough was enough, 
and launched a lawsuit against Adobe.  They claimed “Adobe clearly discloses the 
early termination fee only when subscribers   attempt to cancel, turning the [fee] into 
a powerful retention tool that generates   significant revenues by trapping consumers 
in subscriptions they no longer want.”  Usually, they would just go after the company, 
but Adobe’s fees were so egregious that they went   straight after executives.
Two, in particular:  David Wadhwani, the president of the digital 
media business, and Maninder Sawhney, a senior   vice president in digital sales.
But, it gets worse.  Adobe was desperately trying to capitalize 
on AI, and to do so, they made some   questionable additions to their terms.
They now added a “non-exclusive, worldwide,   royalty-free sublicensable, license, to use, 
reproduce, publicly display”, user’s content. This was especially bad for users with NDAs, like 
those working in the film or commercial space.  Adobe quickly backpedalled, and even after all 
this, their AI bet hadn’t shown much promise.  The stock price was going 
sideways, and soon, downwards. And it was at this time that 
Figma entered the race, for real. On July 31, 2025, Figma went public.
Shares were listed at $33 per share,   at a $19.3 billion valuation.
But the price didn’t stay there.  With so much demand, the price opened at 
$85 a share and jumped as high as $124.  By the end of the day, Figma’s valuation climbed 
to $68 billion, and they had raised $1.2 billion.  To be fair, the share price has 
cooled substantially since then,   but it was still a very successful IPO. 
And Figma wasn’t the only ones celebrating.  Lina Khan, wrote a piece saying
[Lina Khan]: “A great reminder   that letting startups grow into independently 
successful businesses, rather than be bought up   by existing giants, can generate enormous 
value. A win for employees, investors,   innovation, and the public.” (source).
It was the biggest IPO of the year,   and in truth, it was all thanks to Adobe.  Since going public, the share 
price has fallen over 50%.  But in reality, it’s not so dramatic.
The offering was wildly overhyped.  Investors placed 40+ times more 
orders than shares available.  There was a feeding frenzy, and early buyers 
bid up the price into the stratosphere.  Luckily for Figma, this isn’t unusual.
Airbnb and Snowflake had similar IPOs.  So, Figma isn't too worried, but 
there’s still plenty of work to do. Adobe is still a titan, and 
far, far bigger than Figma. But, the goal here isn't actually 
for Figma to win and Adobe to die. Even if you hate Adobe, or Figma, you can’t 
deny, having both is good for everyone.  Markets need competition.
Capitalism is at its best,   when companies compete for customers. When they 
win by offering the best product and service,   not by strangling their competition.
Speaking of competing for customers,   we’re trying to cross 1 million 
subscribers by the end of this year,   and we’re over 80% there! If you hit subscribe 
right now, we might just be able to pull it off. If a company can keep a monopoly by just 
buying and shutting down competitors,   they have no reason to make their products 
the best, or try to win customers.  Instead, they will try to extort them.
They can do whatever they want,   at everyone’s expense.
With the rise of Figma, hopefully the industry   has entered a new era, but only time will tell.
Another fan favorite startup that’s not having   such a good time is Nothing Phone. 
Check out this video to learn more.

## 40. Jack Ma Thought He Was Untouchable...Ended Up Losing $891 Billion

Yet, after this one speech, he vanished.  No IPO, no explanation, and then, nearly $900

years, and why has he returned now? Let’s take a closer look at that speech.

Tension [Jack Ma]: Today’s banks continue to have a   pawnshop mentality… Collaterals 
and warranties are pawnshops.  To make risk-free innovation is 
to stifle innovation… Oftentimes,   managing risk down to zero is the biggest risk.”

yesterday’s way to manage the future.  Even the beggar must have some credit… 
I think every beggar is creditworthy.  Immediately after… Everything went back to normal.

seems perfectly normal.  Compared to how many openly and harshly

senior leaders… including President Xi Jinping.  Little to say, he wasn’t impressed.

Something else concerned him.  Then, the following day, it all began to go wrong.

help young entrepreneurs network and learn.  He also founded “Africa’s Business 
Heroes” under the Jack Ma foundation. It was an entrepreneurship competition, 
which provided winners with funding,   training, and also mentorship.
Jack had become the face of   Chinese success on the global stage.
He was the richest man in China,   charismatic, he had an abundance of data on 
hundreds of millions of Chinese citizens,   his own university, and the 
Chinese youth loved him.  China Daily reported recently that thirty-six 
per cent of college students in Zhejiang   province named Ma as their top role model”.
Jack was hugely popular, and a self-made,   independent success story…
and that was part of the problem. Something else was happening.  The CCP were likely getting 
a bit anxious about Jack Ma.  He was very western, very outspoken, and 
potentially a threat to those in government.  If you’re unsure of the power of a celebrity 
businessman in the world of politics, look no   further than Donald Trump or Elon Musk.
Was Jack scheming for Xi Jinping's   job? I seriously doubt it.
But what mattered was that he was a threat.  And that brings us to Ant Group, Jack’s 
next and arguably most important play. You may not have heard of it, 
but it owns the biggest mobile   and digital payment platform: Alipay.
It’s like Paypal, but also grew into   all kinds of financial services like credit, 
investing, transferring cash, tracking your   credit, even buying insurance.
And its scale is unbelievable.  Alipay at its peak had 1.3 billion users, 
and over 80 million merchants. In total,   it’s processed over $20 trillion in payments.
And most importantly, it was preparing for an   IPO on the Shanghai Stock Exchange.
The biggest IPO in history.  Estimates for AntGroup’s fundraising were 
$34.5 billion, which would value the company   at $313 billion.
And remember,   AntGroup is just one of Jack’s companies.
He had built his wealth when Alibaba went public,   and AntGroup could have been even bigger.
But that didn’t happen.  The timing couldn’t have been worse, 
and next came Jack’s worst nightmare. After Jack’s speech, the reports had 
reached President Xi Jinping himself.  He was deeply troubled with Jack’s comments.  He then decided to halt the 
initial public offering.  The day after Jack Ma was summoned, 
The Shanghai stock exchange suspended   AntGroups's IPO, just days before launch.
Then, an investigation by regulators was underway.  AntGroup began paying back those 
who had initially invested, and  at this time people began 
to notice something else.  Jack had disappeared.
At the grand finals of Africa’s Business Heroes,   Jack was replaced with an Alibaba executive.
It was apparently a “scheduling” conflict,   but something felt off.
As a reminder, Jack was   a very public celebrity. This was odd.
Behind the scenes, things were going from   bad, to worse for Jack. The CCP were 
beginning to dismantle Jack’s empire. In April 2021, Alibaba was hit with a $2.8 
billion fine. The reason? Being a monopoly.  They alleged that Alibaba was forcing 
merchants into using only their platform.  To be fair, this one was true. 
Alibaba was forming a monopoly   in China, similar to Amazon in the West.
But, this wasn’t new. And regulators only   seemed to care after Jack had annoyed them.
Alibaba paid the fine, but the CCP   was was getting started.
That month, they also moved   in and ordered a re-structure of Ant Group, 
and changed it into a financial holding firm.  AntGroup now had to bend to banking 
regulation, not just tech, and had   to hold much more capital in reserve.
It also meant Beijing could monitor it   much closer, and allowed China’s 
big banks to buy into the firm.  Then, they broke up Alipay into multiple 
apps. This was particularly bad, as Alipay   was a super app, and this was a major blow.
Alibaba’s share price tumbled as this happened,   as they held a lot of equity in AntGroup.
But Alibaba had more things to worry about.  UC Browser, Alibaba’s web browser and the #2 
browser in China, was deleted from app stores.  And while Alipay was falling apart, regulators 
were writing new, sweeping reforms on   AntGroup’s most valuable asset: Data.
This was their competitive advantage.   Billions of customer transactions and behavioural 
analytics from hundreds of millions of customers.  The CCP forced Ant to break this “information 
monopoly” and share this with other companies.  Though Ant wasn’t alone.
Nearly all tech giants in   China were coming under fire.
Didi Chuxing, which owns a   taxi and ride-sharing app, had just gone 
public on the NYSE in June 2021, before   regulators launched an investigation into the app.
Didi was removed from all app stores, was delisted   from the NYSE, and was fined $1.2 billion.
It wasn’t allowed to accept new users   until January… 2023.
Then there was ByteDance,   parent company of TikTok.
Not only were recommendation   algorithms added to the “no export list”, but 
their educational app GoGoKid was shut down in   a crackdown on after-school tutoring.
Even Tencent came under fire,   and was fined for antitrust laws.
Jack was at the heart of a new storm, yet,   he somehow had even more things to worry about.
His prestigious business school: Hupan University,   also came under fire. New 
enrollments were suspended,   Ma was forced to disassociate himself, and the 
school had to drop “University” from the name. By mid 2021, Jack’s companies 
had fallen to less than half.  Even investors who bought into Alibaba’s 
IPO were in the red… 10 years later.  Then in 2023, Jack relinquished 
control of AntGroup,   with his voting power dropping from 50%… to 6%.  By 2024, Jack’s companies had 
lost almost $900 billion in value. And during all this time, Jack 
had disappeared from the public.  He stopped giving talks, and had also stopped 
Tweeting altogether, and hasn’t to this day. He has been spotted every so often, 
and was rumoured to be living in Tokyo,   but has all but disappeared.
He was excluded from state media,   including a list of Chinese entrepreneurial 
leaders, despite being the most successful.  Yet, something else has happened in China.
Xi Jinping and the CCP may have made mistakes,   and now, they are desperately trying to rebuild. This sudden, sweeping crackdown had intimidated 
not just Chinese companies… but everyone.  With how easily giants could be 
fined and dismantled, foreign   companies realized China might not be worth it.
In 2024, China’s foreign direct investment (FDI)   fell 29% compared to the previous year.
Walmart sold a stake in one of China’s   e-commerce platforms, worth $3.6 
billion, which it had held for 6 years.  IBM closed a research facility in China.
Honda halted production of three Chinese plants.  Giant US law firms announced the closure 
of offices in Beijing and Hong Kong.  Dell announced it is moving 
manufacturing away from China.  And most recently, Apple has begun moving 
its production to India, alongside Foxconn.  This was bad. And it’s only gotten worse.  In early 2025, fixed asset 
investment grew by only 1.6%.  Industrial output and retail sales 
have also shown a decline in growth.  Consumption was down, the economy was 
slowing, and to make matters worse,   there was a rising youth unemployment rate.
Then, something unexpected happened… On February 17th, 2025, Xi Jinping personally 
hosted a summit for business leaders, at Beijing’s   “Great Hall of the People”.
And there was Jack Ma.  The message was clear: “We need you”.
Just like we need you on this channel.   We’re trying to cross 1 million 
subscribers by the end of this year,   and we’re over 80% there! If you hit subscribe 
right now, we might just be able to pull it off.  One analyst described it as a “strong gesture 
to tell the market and hesitant local officials   that these are our champions that we need to 
unwaveringly support in light of all the risks”  “With many of these entrepreneurs having 
significant stakes in the U.S., Beijing needs a   united front also to prevent major capital flight”
Xi Jinping’s goal was to encourage   entrepreneurship, and rebuild confidence.
But, the damage has already been done.  Many investors have long memories, 
and saw how AntGroup, Didi,   and other entrepreneurs were kneecapped.
China may be proclaiming “we need you”,   but based on what we’ve seen, it comes 
off more as “we need you… for now.”  The irony is that Jack Ma was doing 
the exact same thing in his speech.  If China followed Jack’s advice, 
China would also be better off.  They could’ve offered better, 
modern credit and banking to   give more aspiring entrepreneurs a chance.
Real solutions, not just a display or gesture.  But Jack was cut down for it.
Xi Jinping wants entrepreneurship,   but maybe not too much.
TikTok is feeling the   full brunt of this polarization as

## 41. Was Toyota Right About EVs All Along?
**Channel:** Logically Answered | **Views:** 143K | **Date:** 5 months ago | **Duration:** 14:13 | **ID:** 0n3uG8FrO1A
**Link:** https://youtube.com/watch?v=0n3uG8FrO1A

### Transcript:
Toyota was the first to go big on hybrid cars.  They pioneered the clean 
car movement with the Prius.  But when the world shifted to electric 
vehicles, Toyota… kept building hybrids?  What gives? Wasn’t the world supposed to 
be going all in on all-electric vehicles?  And yet, what Toyota was doing made 
sense. It made all the sense in the world. This video is sponsored by Toyota.
Last year, I made a video exploring   why Toyota was taking things slow in the EV race 
— and why that might actually be a smart move.  After seeing it, Toyota reached out — not to 
promote a specific vehicle, but to go deeper. They   wanted to explore how the landscape has changed, 
why they doubled down on hybrids, and how that   choice fits into their broader “human-first” 
philosophy: building tech not for trendiness,   but to solve real-world problems.
Let’s dive in. Things used to be very, very different.  Instead of being the one behind the 
bandwagon, Toyota was the one pulling it. A quarter century ago, Toyota made 
a huge leap forward with the Prius.  The first mass-produced hybrid vehicle.
It had an internal combustion engine,   but also an electric drive, using both 
electricity and gas to maximize efficiency   and effortlessly breeze by the gas pump.
Toyota didn’t technically “invent” the   hybrid, but they were the pioneers able to 
produce the technology on a massive scale.  It was a breakthrough.
What made it work wasn’t just the tech — it was   the accessibility. Toyota wasn’t trying to create 
a luxury innovation or win headlines. They wanted   a system that regular people could trust. That 
focus on scale and reliability — not hype — shaped   how they’ve approached clean mobility ever since.
Though sometimes being a first   mover means you’re the first loser.
You spend lots and lots of money on R&D, weather   the slow adoption, and then lose when other 
companies push their copies in the mass market.  So what about Toyota?
Hybrids took a few years to take off,   but when they did, it was all the Prius.
In 2004, a total of 84,000 hybrids were   sold in the US, 53,000 of them were the Prius.
Then the next year, the total leapt to 205,000,   and yet still, over half of those 
total sales were the Prius alone.  That trend of dominance continued for 
well over a decade, with the uniquely   egg-shaped Prius seemingly everywhere.
By the time other companies really   started to compete, the Prius 
was so common and so well known,   it became synonymous with the term “hybrid”.
Even as Prius sales began to slow down,   almost 2 decades later, Toyota 
was losing ground… to Toyota. By 2019, virtually every Toyota model 
you could buy had a hybrid option,   from the Camry sedan to the RAV4.  So why was Toyota so dominant 
in Hybrids for so long? New technologies can be risky for consumers. But 
Toyota had built up a reputation for reliability,   which will become important later in this story.
But that’s only part of the reason.  For one, the heart of these machines was 
the “Hybrid Synergy Drive” technology,   which let the engine and motors blend power 
seamlessly, and also: protected by patents.  In order to produce Hybrids, without spending 
billions in R&D, some automakers paid Toyota   for licenses to the technology.
But it was still a big royalty cost.  This kept many brands at bay from 
scaling too fast, and left Toyota   as the undisputed hybrid leader.
But things didn’t stay that way.  Toyota’s iron grip slowly eroded as 
competitors broke into the hybrid market.  By the late 2010s, other automakers were 
starting to gain ground with competitive   hybrid models of their own.
From dropping costs to   expiring patents, the market was opening up.
But then, the market did something strange.  In 2018, Toyota had a 9% global market 
share of hybrids. In 2024, it jumped to 37%.  How?
What happened in these few years? Well, many legacy automakers became sidetracked.
For a long time, the move towards cleaner   vehicles had been inevitable. Or 
at least, away from fossil fuels.  Other automakers saw Hybrids as just a 
stepping stone. All the while, Toyota   continued to improve its Hybrids, making 
them more efficient and more accessible.  For Toyota, hybrids weren’t a placeholder. 
They were — and still are — a core solution   for reducing emissions while keeping 
cars affordable and usable worldwide and   part of a multi-pathway strategy to provide 
choices for customers of all walks of life. From others’ perspectives, once it became 
clear that electric vehicles could deliver   explosive growth, legacy carmakers jumped 
in headfirst to chase that same trajectory.  Several major automakers committed 
to selling only EVs by 2030.  Companies were throwing piles of money at 
electric cars. Battery production, research,   all kinds of manufacturing. 
Anything and everything.  It almost felt like an arms race. 
To see who could build the biggest   EV factory and dominate the fastest.
But were EVs what was really best for   the customer? Did it meet their varying needs 
and had enough been done to ease their minds?  Despite strong government support, there 
were still charging deserts - places where   a lot of people lived, but had no access 
to charging at home. While there were more   options for customers to purchase an EV, 
there were not a lot of affordable options.  More incentives were added, and larger and 
larger numbers of battery EVs were being sold. The EV market did explode… for a while. But 
demand didn’t continue like everyone hoped.  By 2024, it was clear that the EV 
gold rush was beginning to slow.   Tides shifted. The speculation of where the 
market was heading came back down to earth.  Despite rising numbers in 
sales, losses were piling up.  Spending was slashed. EV production 
targets were quietly walked back.   Entire models were scrapped before launch.
The promise of a fully electric automobile   market by 2030 quietly pushed those 
goals out. A few still held firm on   their original targets — but with losses 
mounting, the tension was building.  Meanwhile, startups were collapsing left and 
right — companies that once made headlines   for “disrupting” the car industry ended up 
folding under the weight of production costs,   infrastructure gaps, and weak demand – 
the reality of fragile capital markets.  At the same time, one thing never really 
changed: EVs were still expensive.   The price gap between electric and gas-powered 
cars hadn’t closed the way people expected.  So buyers started looking elsewhere — and 
hybrids quietly slipped back into the mainstream.  What used to be seen as a compromise 
suddenly felt like a smart middle ground.  This isn’t an “EVs are bad” story. Far 
from it. Electric vehicles are amazing   for the right person, in the right place.
But it’s clear they aren’t going to fix all   our problems and aren’t for everyone.
Some governments that heavily pushed   EVs began to backpedal, which 
raised the effective price of EVs.  With softening EV demand and Toyota’s hybrid 
sales continuing to grow, that brings us   back to a looming question:
Did Toyota see this coming?  If Toyota was so early with hybrids, 
why were they so far behind with EVs?  Surely they had the technology and a head start.
Let’s delve a little deeper. Toyota is a methodical company.
Rather than rush into the next big   thing, Toyota took a deep breath, 
and listened to its customers. Toyota was in the news for insisting that hybrids 
were a more appropriate focus. One might think,   “of course they’d say that, they own the Hybrid 
market”, but their rationale does make sense.  Hybrids reduce emissions by 30%. 
Individually a lot less than an EV,   depending on how the electricity is generated.
But on the macro scale, the tables seem to turn.  Think about it this way — if each hybrid cuts 
emissions by around 30%, and you had enough   battery material for one EV or 20 hybrids, those 
hybrids could collectively reduce far more total   emissions and fuel use than that one EV.
This is because batteries use lithium,   and smaller batteries in hybrids mean more cars.
It's an extremely compelling reason for   hybrids. But there’s more.
If you live in Urban America,   EVs typically struggle from a lack 
of access to charging stations. You   also have to deal with stations not working.
Hybrids, however, still work well, even in areas   where there is no easy access to public charging.
The conundrum can be summarized in a response   to “EV only policies.” Toyota’s current chairman 
and grandson of Toyota’s founder, Akio Toyoda said   “Because the right answer is still unclear, we 
shouldn’t limit ourselves to just one option”.  While it may seem like incredible foresight 
given what happened, especially compared to   automakers who are still committed to 
removing all internal combustion cars,   it was really about listening to the customer, 
even when the customer might seem silent.  Toyoda elaborated: "The silent majority 
is wondering whether EVs are really OK to   have as a single option. But they think it's 
the trend so they can't speak out loudly.”  Clearly, customers had different opinions 
than what was being popularized in the press,   and even if they didn’t say it out loud, their 
pocketbooks were speaking. Though it may have   looked like hesitation, it was clear that 
by waiting and watching, a move that doesn’t   always look innovative — over time showed that 
it can be the most forward-thinking move of all.  While Toyota’s focus was on hybrids, 
they kept an eye on other technologies   and continued developing vehicles with 
them — like battery EVs and hydrogen.  Toyota’s well-stated belief that no single 
technology will solve climate change for   everyone was rooted in a global understanding 
that different regions face different challenges.  While some technologies, like Hydrogen fuel cell 
electric vehicles haven’t taken off the same way   EVs or hybrids have, Toyota’s interest in them 
as potential solutions based on customers’ needs   shows they’ve never been all-in on a single bet.
Instead, their philosophy has always been to   prepare and offer a line-up of options to 
meet different needs, in different places,   with a long-term view of what really works.
And these efforts are actually paying off. Even with the slowdown of EVs, 
you might think hybrids are   starting to leave the market as EVs grow.
But, in reality, the opposite has happened. Hybrid sales in 2023 leapt to almost 1.2 
million sales in the US, up 53% from 2022. Globally, that number is over 4 million.
Hybrids are even growing more than EVs. The more modern plug-in hybrids are growing too.
The “electric battery market” is growing   massively, but people often attribute that to EVs.
But in reality, it's the hybrids   doing the heavy lifting.
Even with EV sales growing,   they’re still quite niche.
That brings us back to Toyota.  The hybrid share of Toyota’s global 
sales jumped from 9 % in 2018 to 37 %   by mid‑2024. By the end of 2024, it had 
surpassed 40% - an unbelievable jump.  While competitors spent all their money on EVs, 
Toyota relentlessly worked on their hybrids,   and moved into the gaps left by others.
Over just the past year, Toyota has made quiet   but important upgrades to its hybrid lineup.
They improved battery technology, improving   performance by 10% and fuel economy by 
20%. Those same vehicles saw jumps in   horsepower and enhancements in aerodynamics 
and other things that made them more fun to   drive and more appealing to customers.
While legacy companies were looking to   the far future, Toyota was working 
to solve customer problems today.  Many people were ready to leave combustion 
engines, but not willing to jump to EVs.  Hybrids are a middle ground solution, one 
that still helps reduce carbon dioxide   emissions. And they are bound to stick 
around much longer than people expected. Now, Toyota looks like it may have 
been right all along, relying on its   patience and listening to the customer’s voice.
For a company that’s supposedly “slow to EVs”,   they sure are doing well to 
listen to their customers.  Everyone leaving the hybrid market behind 
allowed Toyota to dominate, and now they   hold the hybrid market with an iron grip.
There’s so much growing demand for hybrids   that suppliers can’t keep up.
Now, legacy companies are   beginning to turn back to hybrids.
Many legacy automakers saw hybrids   as the stepping stone to EVs, but Toyota 
saw it as part of the lineup alongside EVs.  The current CEO Koji Sato said “In the next few 
years we will expand our line-up in the important   battery electric category… but [there is no 
doubt] hybrids remain a core pillar for us”.  Maybe now is the time for Toyota 
to really enter the EV race.  They moved first to hybrids and 
were a first winner, but with EVs,   they avoided the massive losses and investments 
that currently burden other automakers.  Perhaps they’ll produce more EVs soon, 
but one thing is clear for Toyota:  Slow and steady wins the race. They didn’t 
chase the future — they built toward it,   piece by piece. And now, the rest of the 
world is coming to the same realization.  To learn more about Toyota’s approach 
to electrification — from hybrids to   hydrogen and beyond — check out the link below.  But until then, I’m Hari, and 
I’ll see you guys on the next one.

Well, that also brings us to Son's other problems.  Just two years in, he was under a bit of pressure.
The Vision Fund had made huge bets,   but there were no real returns.
Some startups like Uber were growing,   though still bleeding through cash. 
Others were showing troubling losses,   and weren't growing as fast as Son had hoped.
To make matters worse, in late 2018, Nvidia   had also dropped after the crypto mining crash.
Son needed to show a return, especially since   he was planning a sequel: the Vision 
Fund 2, which would need investment.  So, with a 4.7x ROI locked in, Son sold Nvidia… 
A decision he'd regret for the rest of his life.  But unfortunately, this wouldn't be 
the end of the Vision Fund troubles.  As it turns out, some of the companies 
in the Vision Fund were a bit… strange.  One of these was WeWork. It was right at home in the "crowd 
sharing" explosion, like Airbnb and Uber.  It was a tech startup that leased office 
space to other startups, freelancers,   or those who needed co-working space.
Except, it didn't own any real estate.  It just subleased the office 
space out to other companies.  What's even stranger is the CEO, Adam 
Neumann, and how he got his funding.  He gave Masayoshi Son a 12-minute 
tour, who then invited him to his   car. He then sketched a deal on his iPad.
An investment of $4.4 billion in WeWork.  He told Neumann to make WeWork "ten times bigger 
than your original plan' and to recognize that, in   a fight, being crazy is better than being smart, 
and that WeWork wasn't being 'crazy enough."  I suppose Adam smelled like an entrepreneur.
In total, SoftBank poured around $10-12 billion   into WeWork, some of which 
came from the Vision Fund.  But the other investors weren't too happy 
about this, and their hesitation was soon   justified with the WeWork IPO.
As the details came out,   investors realized Adam Neumann had been doing 
some… questionable things in his startup. With a sudden multi-billion dollar investment,   and based on Son’s suggestion…
He went a bit crazy.  For one, he trademarked the word "We," then 
sold it to his own company for about $6 million.  He cashed out $700 million in 
stock, before the IPO even listed.  He structured the company so he 
owned "super-shares," each having   20 votes. Even after the IPO, Adam would 
control at least 50% of voting rights.  He also spent millions on a private jet.
All of that is on top of an arbitrary $47 billion   valuation, for a company that was bleeding cash.
Remember, they didn't own any real estate,   they just leased it.
Even with billions from Masayoshi Son,   the financials were looking troubling.
I suppose if you pour $10 billion into   an unproven, unprofitable startup, based on

retire, and of course, that private jet was sold.  There was even crazier stuff going on at 
WeWork, that someone wrote an entire book on it.  Even after one of his biggest bets had failed,

problems, it was nearly all of his investments. Over the years, we’ve amassed 170 million

It was still losing billions every year.  Investors in the Vision Fund were 
starting to get impatient, and many   were pushing for more transparency and change.
But little change came; instead, came the losses.  As 2020 rolled around, many of these fragile, 
cash-burning start-ups collapsed in the pandemic.  Even companies you'd think would thrive 
here, like Uber, saw a huge drop in revenue.  In May 2020, SoftBank reported 
the fund had lost $18 billion,   with many of the fund's staff being laid off.
Son had dug himself into a hole, yet,   for some reason, he decided to keep digging.
On an investor call, he compared himself to Jesus.  He said that "just like 
Jesus, he is misunderstood."  He called his WeWork investment 
foolish, but said that if the   "performance of his investments improved, 
his reputation would improve as well."  Despite his efforts, this didn't help his 
reputation, which he needed for his next big plan. Remember the big sequel? The Vision Fund 2? Well, 
there wasn't as much excitement this time around.  Masayoshi Son had a $108 billion goal for 
this fund, but it raised less than half.  Even worse, it was all funded by SoftBank itself.
Son couldn't secure any external investors.  No one wanted in on this disaster.
But, there was a glimpse of hope.  To everyone's surprise, one of the 
Vision Fund start-ups brought a return.  Coupang.
The ecommerce platform was exploding,   and was called the "Amazon of South Korea."
$2 billion was invested from the Vision Fund,   and Coupang had a gigantic IPO, 
reaching $85 billion in market cap.  This was a near $37 billion profit.
But Masayoshi Son didn't have time to celebrate.  Despite this huge profit, it was only 
a small chunk of the Vision Fund,   which was overwhelmingly underperforming.
Even with Coupang's return and their now   profitability, it wasn't enough.
Uber was still losing billions,   and when they finally did make 
profit, investors were underwhelmed.  WeWork finally performed an IPO in 2021, 
and reached a market cap of $8 billion,   far, far below Son's original valuation, marking 
a colossal loss. And it only went down from there. The other companies were much the same. Shedding 
cash left and right, or just collapsing like FTX.  In 2022, SoftBank reported a $27.4 billion loss 
for the Vision Fund, for the previous year.  Its portfolio of stocks was plummeting.
Then, things began to accelerate.  The fund lost $23 billion, just 
for the April–June quarter alone.  By the end of that year, 
they had lost $32 billion.  Not even OpenAI's explosion could save the fund.
SoftBank began scaling back their investments,   and in the third quarter, only 
invested $300 million in new startups.  Sounds like a lot, but that's just 10% 
of what they invested the previous year.  But it was too late.  Investors had absolutely 
had it with Masayoshi Son.  He may have made some great bets in 
the past, but he was being reckless.  The Wall Street Journal called SoftBank a "big 
loser", and Bloomberg described the fund as   "Masayoshi Son's broken business model."
He was even called a gambling addict. Son's dream fund had collapsed.
The one company that could have saved the Vision   Fund, and made it all worth it, was NVIDIA.
Son was humiliated, but at least   all of this had humbled him.
"I am ashamed of myself for being so   elated by big profits in the past," he said.
He was arrogant and prideful about his past   success, notably that huge Alibaba return.
But, going back to his own example, as Jesus said,   "those who exalt themselves will be humbled."
Maybe he’ll change his mindset moving forward,   but only time will tell.
